Ways of the World · Second Wave Civilizations

Unit 2 Essay Prompt Set

Twenty-two paths into the arguments historians still have about the empires, faiths, and societies of the second-wave world.

Chapters 3, 4, 5 & 6 · 600 B.C.E.–1200 C.E.

Research begins with a real question

Choose an argument worth testing.

Each prompt pairs a question historians genuinely disagree about with defensible positions and a source map. Choose one prompt and one numbered position, refine it into your own thesis, and use the mapped evidence as a launch point, not a checklist.

22Essay prompts
68Defensible positions

Writer’s guide

From question to historical argument

01

Choose

Select one prompt and one numbered position. You may refine the position, but your thesis must answer the prompt directly.

02

Research

Build your argument from primary evidence and historians’ scholarship. Let the source map point you toward the conversations worth joining.

03

Cite

Each identifier opens the source in The Garza Archive. Cite the original book, journal, or web pages shown there, never the reader’s page numbers.

04

Interpret

Supports, qualifies, challenges, and counters describe how a source can help you. A source that counters your position is the Counter-Argument you must answer.

Read the note on contested positions
  • 3.1.1 · Persia Through Whose Eyes? — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Argue from consequences, not caricature: whatever Persia was like, a Persian victory would have ended the independent citizen-state in which Athenian democracy grew. Use the Funeral Oration and Green, and answer Hall's point that the 'slavish barbarian' was an Athenian invention. Start with: S3.21, S3.37, S3.38, A3.29, A3.37, A3.60.
  • 3.2.1 · The Verdict on Qin — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Keep the position but test it: use Han Fei and the tomb complex for what Qin demanded of its people, then show that Han writers had reasons to exaggerate. The strongest version rests on labor and conscription burdens, which Qin's own documents confirm. Start with: S3.24, S3.40, S3.41, A3.57, A3.54, A3.55.
  • 3.3.3 · Ashoka's Dhamma — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Build it from pieces: Thapar's revised model of uneven Mauryan control (metropolitan, core, and peripheral zones) and Coningham and Young's archaeology of the empire's reach. Start with: S3.28, S3.43, S3.8, S3.42, A3.44, A3.46.
  • 3.5.3 · The End of the Western Roman Empire — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Use Harper to argue that disease and climate made fifth-century crises unmanageable, and be explicit about which evidence is western and fifth-century. Answer Halsall or Heather, who do not give environment a central role. Start with: S3.14, S3.32, S3.46, S3.54, A3.19, A3.33.
  • 3.6.1 · Why China Reunified and Rome Did Not — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Build the comparison yourself: Pines for the Chinese unity ideal and literati, and the western absence of an equivalent official class loyal to a single imperial center (Wickham), with Scheidel as the counter-argument to answer. Start with: S3.16, S3.31, S3.4, S3.20, S3.35, S3.52.
  • 4.2.2 · Confucianism and Imperial Power — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Distinguish a standard for criticizing rulers from a mechanism that could restrain them, and ask whether Han officials ever succeeded. Start with: S4.28, S4.29, S4.4, A4.1, A3.59, A4.21.
  • 4.2.3 · Confucianism and Imperial Power — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Test the claim against the Han statutes: find how much Qin-style law and punishment survives, then ask whether Confucian ideas changed practice or only language. Start with: S4.19, S4.5, S4.6, S4.28, S4.4, A4.37.
  • 4.3.1 · Buddhism and the Brahmin Tradition — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Separate spiritual equality (the sangha open to all varnas; worth by conduct, not birth) from social revolution (abolishing caste), and argue which the texts show. Start with: S4.20, S4.21, S4.32, S4.8, A3.1, A3.46.
  • 4.4.3 · Why Did Christianity Spread? — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Decide whether growth before or after 312 matters more. Use Stark's numbers against MacMullen's post-312 story. Start with: S4.13, S4.38, S4.23, A4.2, A4.3, A4.15.
  • 5.1.1 · Explaining Caste — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Argue that religious ideas made the hierarchy legitimate and durable (how it was held together), rather than that they created it, and acknowledge Dumont's modern evidence base. Start with: S5.1, S5.22, S5.42, A5.13, A5.19, A5.29.
  • 5.2.3 · State, Landlords, and Peasants in Han China — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Argue that uprisings were decisive at two turning points (the end of Wang Mang's regime, c. 23 C.E., and the crisis of 184 C.E.), and show how each forced the state and landlords to change course, instead of claiming constant control from below. Start with: S5.26, S5.27, S5.7, A5.35, A5.15, A5.22.
  • 5.4.1 · Comparing Patriarchies — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Argue shared structure (patrilineal inheritance, control of women's sexuality, male-authored normative texts) and support it with the supplied texts (Manu, Lessons for Women, Oeconomica), and drop or qualify the 'more restrictive over time' claim. Start with: S5.20, S5.21, S5.38, S5.39, S5.36, S5.40.
  • 6.1.1 · Isolated or Connected? — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Build 6.1.3 on Teotihuacan-Maya and Pacific voyaging; leave the Andes to the textbook. Start with: S6.17, S6.2, S6.18, S6.3, S6.43, A6.13.
  • 6.1.3 · Isolated or Connected? — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Build 6.1.3 on Teotihuacan-Maya and Pacific voyaging; leave the Andes to the textbook. Start with: S6.14, S6.17, S6.33, S6.4, S6.44, S6.40.
  • 6.2.3 · Is "Civilization" the Right Yardstick? — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Argue 'choice' only where the record shows hierarchy tried and then undone (Teotihuacan after about 350 C.E.; Chaco and Cahokia's aftermath), and admit that intention is inferred. Start with: S6.10, S6.20, S6.21, S6.6, S6.15, S6.39.
  • 6.4.2 · Power at Teotihuacan — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Use the absence of ruler portraits and the apartment compounds as evidence, but address Sugiyama's sacrificial burials. The honest version of 6.4.2 covers the city's height (about 250-550 C.E.). Start with: S6.48, S6.49, S6.10, S6.27, A6.36, A6.41.
  • 6.6.3 · What Held North America's Great Centers Together? — Few historians argue this position in exactly this form, so build it from their findings and say plainly that you are doing so. Pair Hopewell (the textbook and Yerkes in Recreating Hopewell) with Chaco read through Van Dyke, and argue that shared ceremony and exchange, not permanent rulers, linked communities. Start with: S6.15, S6.34, S6.38, S6.47, S6.50, A6.4.

Chapter 3 · 600 B.C.E.–600 C.E.

3.1 · Persia Through Whose Eyes?

What kind of empire did the Achaemenid Persians build, and how far can the Greek picture of it be trusted?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

What kind of empire did the Achaemenid Persians build, and how far can the Greek picture of it be trusted?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 3.1.1 A despotism the Greeks were right to resist: Persian rule rested on the unchecked will of a "king of kings," and the Greek victories of 490-479 B.C.E. preserved a genuinely different political world of citizens rather than subjects. Contested
  2. 3.1.2 A tolerant, pragmatic imperial system: Persia held its vast territory by respecting local religions, laws, and elites and by efficient administration; the Greek image of despotism is largely a caricature built for Greek audiences.
  3. 3.1.3 An ideology of universal rule: Persian kings presented conquest as a sacred mission of Truth against "the Lie," and their famous tolerance was a tool of that ideology rather than an alternative to it.
Keep in focus: Stay within 550-330 B.C.E. Test Greek accounts against evidence the Persians made themselves (inscriptions, reliefs, administrative texts), not against Herodotus alone.
In Plain Terms

Main question: What was the Persian Empire really like, and can we believe what the Greeks said about it?

  • Option 1: Persia was a one-man dictatorship, and the Greeks who beat it saved a freer way of life.
  • Option 2: Persia ruled smartly and tolerantly by letting local peoples keep their gods, laws, and leaders; the Greeks exaggerated.
  • Option 3: Persian kings believed a god told them to rule the world; their tolerance was part of that claim to total power.

Source Map

Every source in the four readers that bears on this prompt, with how it relates to each position: supports, qualifies, challenges, or counters (a Counter-Argument to answer). Reader pages are the printed page numbers in the reader. Select an identifier to open the source in The Garza Archive.

Prompt 1 of 22

Chapter 3 · 600 B.C.E.–600 C.E.

3.2 · The Verdict on Qin

How should historians judge the Qin dynasty (221-206 B.C.E.)?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

How should historians judge the Qin dynasty (221-206 B.C.E.)?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 3.2.1 A tyranny undone by its own brutality: Legalist harshness, forced labor, and suppression of learning turned the population against the dynasty, which is why it collapsed within fifteen years. Contested
  2. 3.2.2 The founder of imperial China: whatever its cruelty, Qin standardization, centralized administration, and law created the template the Han and later dynasties kept, even while condemning Qin in public.
  3. 3.2.3 A reputation shaped by its successors: excavated Qin legal and administrative documents reveal a rule-bound bureaucracy that also sought cooperation, and the image of pure tyranny owes much to Han-era writers who needed Qin to be the villain.
Keep in focus: Stay within 221-206 B.C.E., plus Han-era judgments of Qin to about 90 B.C.E. Separate what Qin did from what later writers said Qin did.
In Plain Terms

Main question: Was the Qin dynasty a cruel failure, a necessary builder, or a victim of bad press?

  • Option 1: Qin was so harsh that people turned against it, so it fell fast.
  • Option 2: Qin was harsh, but it built the system of government China kept for 2,000 years.
  • Option 3: Qin was more organized and less monstrous than we think; the Han dynasty that replaced it painted it as evil.

Source Map

Every source in the four readers that bears on this prompt, with how it relates to each position: supports, qualifies, challenges, or counters (a Counter-Argument to answer). Reader pages are the printed page numbers in the reader. Select an identifier to open the source in The Garza Archive.

Primary Sources (5)

ReaderSource and citationPositions

Chapter 3 · 600 B.C.E.–600 C.E.

3.3 · Ashoka's Dhamma

What was Ashoka's dhamma?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

What was Ashoka's dhamma?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations of the Mauryan emperor's policy (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 3.3.1 A genuine moral and religious transformation: remorse after Kalinga and a sincere Buddhist commitment reshaped how Ashoka ruled.
  2. 3.3.2 A deliberate strategy of state: dhamma was a broad, non-sectarian civic ethic designed to bind a religiously and culturally diverse empire to its ruler.
  3. 3.3.3 Royal rhetoric larger than reality: the edicts tell us what Ashoka wanted his subjects to hear, while the empire's actual reach was uneven, coercion remained available, and the Mauryan state fragmented soon after his death. Contested
Keep in focus: Stay within 268-185 B.C.E. Treat the edicts as the emperor's self-presentation, and flag any later Buddhist legend you use as a later source.
In Plain Terms

Main question: Why did Ashoka promote dhamma, and did it really change his empire?

  • Option 1: Ashoka truly changed after the Kalinga war and ruled as a sincere Buddhist.
  • Option 2: Ashoka used dhamma as a smart way to unite many different peoples under his rule.
  • Option 3: Ashoka's messages sounded better than his empire actually worked, and the empire fell apart soon after.

Prompt 3 of 22
3

Chapter 3 · 600 B.C.E.–600 C.E.

3.4 · What Held Empires Together?

What most effectively held the great second-wave empires together?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

What most effectively held the great second-wave empires together?

Choose and defend one of the following explanations, comparing at least two empires (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 3.4.1 Force: armies, garrisons, and the credible threat of terrible punishment kept subjects in line.
  2. 3.4.2 Bargains with local elites: empires survived by co-opting provincial aristocracies, sharing citizenship or office, and leaving local law and religion largely alone.
  3. 3.4.3 Ideology and consent: rulers won acceptance by claiming divine sanction or moral virtue and by cultivating loyalty through images, ritual, and public messages.
  4. 3.4.4 Administrative and fiscal machinery: bureaucracies, taxation, roads, coinage, and standardization gave empires a reach that no single army or idea could.
Keep in focus: Stay within about 550 B.C.E.-220 C.E. and compare at least two of Persia, Rome, Han China, and Mauryan India. Name the single most important "glue" rather than listing every factor.
In Plain Terms

Main question: What was the most important thing that kept huge empires from falling apart?

  • Option 1: Military power and fear of punishment.
  • Option 2: Deals with local leaders who got to keep power if they cooperated.
  • Option 3: Beliefs and propaganda that made people accept the ruler as legitimate.
  • Option 4: Government systems such as officials, taxes, roads, and standard money.

Source Map

Every source in the four readers that bears on this prompt, with how it relates to each position: supports, qualifies, challenges, or counters (a Counter-Argument to answer). Reader pages are the printed page numbers in the reader. Select an identifier to open the source in The Garza Archive.

Chapter 3 · 600 B.C.E.–600 C.E.

3.5 · The End of the Western Roman Empire

What best explains the end of the western Roman Empire in the fifth century C.E.?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

What best explains the end of the western Roman Empire in the fifth century C.E.?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 3.5.1 External pressure and violent catastrophe: invading and migrating peoples, set in motion by the Huns, dismantled the western empire, and the result was a sharp collapse in living standards and complexity.
  2. 3.5.2 Internal political breakdown: civil wars, usurpations, and the army's role in making and unmaking emperors weakened the western state from within before outsiders took advantage.
  3. 3.5.3 Environment and disease: climate change and pandemics eroded Rome's population, revenue, and resilience, turning manageable crises into unmanageable ones. Contested
  4. 3.5.4 Transformation, not fall: the fifth-century West saw Roman and "barbarian" elites accommodate one another in new kingdoms, and late antiquity was an era of creative change rather than an end.
Keep in focus: Stay within about 235-600 C.E. Make clear whether you are explaining why the western empire ended or judging whether its ending was a disaster, and do not confuse the western empire with the eastern (Byzantine) empire.
In Plain Terms

Main question: Why did the western Roman Empire end, and was that a disaster?

  • Option 1: Outside groups invaded, and life got much worse afterward.
  • Option 2: Romans fought each other so much that the government broke down on its own.
  • Option 3: Climate change and plagues weakened Rome until it could not recover.
  • Option 4: Rome did not really "fall"; it slowly changed into new kingdoms that kept much of Roman life.

Prompt 5 of 22

Chapter 3 · 600 B.C.E.–600 C.E.

3.6 · Why China Reunified and Rome Did Not

After both the Han and the western Roman empires broke apart, China was reunified under the Sui (589 C.E.) while the western Mediterranean never again came under one ruler.

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

After both the Han and the western Roman empires broke apart, China was reunified under the Sui (589 C.E.) while the western Mediterranean never again came under one ruler.

Choose and defend one of the following explanations (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 3.6.1 Culture and ideology: a shared written language, a Confucian-trained official class, and the ideal of one ruler over "all under Heaven" kept political unity thinkable and desirable in China. Contested
  2. 3.6.2 Geography and the steppe frontier: north China's exposure to the steppe repeatedly produced powerful conquest states able to reunify the country, while Europe's geography and weaker frontier pressure favored lasting fragmentation.
  3. 3.6.3 Rival institutions in the West: the Church, entrenched landed aristocracies, and successor kingdoms created durable power centers that no would-be emperor could absorb.
Keep in focus: Stay within 220-600 C.E. Compare China and the western Mediterranean directly; do not explain only one side.
In Plain Terms

Main question: Why did China get put back together while the western Roman world stayed divided?

  • Option 1: Chinese culture, writing, and ideas about one ruler kept people wanting unity.
  • Option 2: Geography and pressure from northern nomads pushed China toward reunification but not Europe.
  • Option 3: In the West, the Church and powerful landowners and kings held too much independent power for anyone to unite them.

Chapter 4 · 600 B.C.E.–600 C.E.

4.1 · Was There an Axial Age?

Did China, India, the Middle East, and Greece share a common "Axial Age" transformation between about 800 and 200 B.C.E.?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

Did China, India, the Middle East, and Greece share a common "Axial Age" transformation between about 800 and 200 B.C.E.?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 4.1.1 A real shared breakthrough: across Eurasia, thinkers turned toward a transcendent source of order and meaning, universal ethics, and critical self-reflection, a genuine turning point in human thought.
  2. 4.1.2 A real pattern with material causes: iron, cities, money, warfare, and new wealth produced similar social pressures in several regions, and the new traditions were answers to those pressures rather than a mysterious simultaneous awakening.
  3. 4.1.3 A modern scholarly construct: the label groups unlike traditions together, stretches or ignores chronology, and imposes a European template on very different histories.
Keep in focus: Stay within about 800-200 B.C.E. (Christianity only as a later comparison). Compare at least three of the four regions and engage the chapter's Controversies feature on the Axial Age.
In Plain Terms

Main question: Did several civilizations really go through the same big change in religion and philosophy at about the same time?

  • Option 1: Yes, people in many places started thinking in deep new ways about goodness and a higher reality.
  • Option 2: Yes, but because of practical changes such as cities, iron, money, and war, not a shared spiritual awakening.
  • Option 3: No, modern scholars invented the idea by lumping very different traditions together.

Chapter 4 · 600 B.C.E.–600 C.E.

4.2 · Confucianism and Imperial Power

In Han China, how did Confucianism function in relation to imperial power?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

In Han China, how did Confucianism function in relation to imperial power?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 4.2.1 An ideology of hierarchy: Han Confucianism legitimized the emperor as Heaven's agent and justified the privileges of fathers, husbands, and officials.
  2. 4.2.2 A moral check on rulers: Confucian ideals such as the Mandate of Heaven, the duty to remonstrate, and government by virtue gave officials and scholars standards by which to criticize and restrain the throne. Contested
  3. 4.2.3 Confucian on the outside, Legalist within: the Han kept Qin methods (law, punishment, state monopolies) beneath Confucian rhetoric, so Confucianism served mainly as a respectable public face. Contested
Keep in focus: Stay within 206 B.C.E.-220 C.E. Focus on how the Han state and its officials used Confucian ideas, not only on what Confucius himself taught.
In Plain Terms

Main question: Did Confucian ideas support the emperor, limit the emperor, or just cover up harsher methods?

  • Option 1: Confucianism taught people to obey and made the emperor and elites look legitimate.
  • Option 2: Confucianism gave officials a way to judge and criticize bad rulers.
  • Option 3: Han rulers talked like Confucians but governed with Legalist laws and punishments.

Source Map

Every source in the four readers that bears on this prompt, with how it relates to each position: supports, qualifies, challenges, or counters (a Counter-Argument to answer). Reader pages are the printed page numbers in the reader. Select an identifier to open the source in The Garza Archive.

Chapter 4 · 600 B.C.E.–600 C.E.

4.3 · Buddhism and the Brahmin Tradition

How should we understand the relationship between early Buddhism and the Brahmin (Vedic) tradition of classical India?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

How should we understand the relationship between early Buddhism and the Brahmin (Vedic) tradition of classical India?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 4.3.1 A revolt against Brahmin privilege: Buddhism rejected Vedic sacrifice, priestly authority, and birth-based caste, making it a socially radical challenge to the existing order. Contested
  2. 4.3.2 A reform within a shared Indian framework: the Buddha accepted karma, rebirth, and the goal of liberation, and engaged Brahmin ideas critically from inside a common world of thought.
  3. 4.3.3 A separate root: Buddhism grew from a distinct, non-Vedic religious culture in the eastern Ganges region ("Greater Magadha"), which the Brahmin tradition later absorbed and reworked.
Keep in focus: Stay within about 600 B.C.E.-300 C.E. Compare Buddhist texts with Brahmin texts directly rather than relying on later summaries of "Hinduism."
In Plain Terms

Main question: Was Buddhism a rebellion against the Brahmin religion, a reform of it, or something that came from a different source?

  • Option 1: Buddhism rebelled against priests and the caste system.
  • Option 2: Buddhism kept the same basic ideas (karma, rebirth) but tried to fix and improve the older religion.
  • Option 3: Buddhism came from a different regional culture, and Brahmin religion later borrowed from it.

Chapter 4 · 600 B.C.E.–600 C.E.

4.4 · Why Did Christianity Spread?

How did a small Jewish movement become the dominant religion of the Roman Empire by about 400 C.E.?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

How did a small Jewish movement become the dominant religion of the Roman Empire by about 400 C.E.?

Choose and defend one of the following explanations (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 4.4.1 Community and social networks: Christians grew through family and friendship ties, mutual care during epidemics, and a strong appeal to women, steadily compounding over three centuries.
  2. 4.4.2 A distinctive message: belief in one God, universal salvation, exclusive commitment, and demanding ethics set Christianity apart from Roman religion and gave converts something new.
  3. 4.4.3 Imperial power: Constantine's conversion, state patronage, and later legal pressure under Theodosius turned a minority faith into the majority religion. Contested
Keep in focus: Stay within about 30-400 C.E. Decide whether growth before Constantine (312 C.E.) or after him matters more to your argument, and say so.
In Plain Terms

Main question: Why did Christianity grow from a tiny group into the main religion of the Roman Empire?

  • Option 1: Christians spread the faith through relationships and by caring for each other, especially during plagues.
  • Option 2: Christianity's beliefs were new and attractive compared with Roman religion.
  • Option 3: Emperors adopted Christianity and used government power to spread it.

Source Map

Prompt 10 of 22
4

Chapter 4 · 600 B.C.E.–600 C.E.

4.5 · Did States Make World Religions?

Did Buddhism, Christianity, and Confucianism become major traditions mainly because states adopted them?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

Did Buddhism, Christianity, and Confucianism become major traditions mainly because states adopted them?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations, comparing at least two traditions (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 4.5.1 State sponsorship was decisive: rulers such as Ashoka, Constantine, and Han Wudi gave these traditions the resources, protection, and reach they could not have gained on their own.
  2. 4.5.2 Other carriers did the spreading: merchants, monks, missionaries, and ordinary communities carried these traditions along trade routes and through local networks, and states joined movements already under way.
  3. 4.5.3 The alliance changed the religions: state patronage brought hierarchy, official orthodoxy, and bitter disputes over doctrine, reshaping the traditions themselves as much as spreading them.
Keep in focus: Stay within about 268 B.C.E.-600 C.E. Compare at least two traditions, and distinguish a tradition's growth from its transformation.
In Plain Terms

Main question: Did governments make these religions big, or did they spread in other ways?

  • Option 1: Rulers who supported these religions were the main reason they grew.
  • Option 2: Traders, monks, and everyday believers spread them first; governments came later.
  • Option 3: Government support changed the religions themselves, adding official rules, leaders, and fights over beliefs.

Source Map

Every source in the four readers that bears on this prompt, with how it relates to each position: supports, qualifies, challenges, or counters (a Counter-Argument to answer). Reader pages are the printed page numbers in the reader. Select an identifier to open the source in The Garza Archive.

5

Chapter 5 · 600 B.C.E.–600 C.E.

5.1 · Explaining Caste

What made caste the organizing principle of classical Indian society?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

What made caste the organizing principle of classical Indian society?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 5.1.1 Religious ideas of purity and duty: caste rested on a religious logic of purity and pollution, dharma, and karma that ranked every group and made the hierarchy feel sacred. Contested
  2. 5.1.2 Economic power and control of labor: caste organized who did what work and who controlled land and surplus, with religious language justifying arrangements that served dominant groups.
  3. 5.1.3 More fluid than the texts admit: law books such as the Laws of Manu describe an ideal, while lived jati groups were regional, negotiable, and hardened only gradually over centuries.
Keep in focus: Stay within about 1000 B.C.E.-600 C.E. Treat texts such as the Laws of Manu as prescriptions, and explain what they can and cannot tell us about actual practice.
In Plain Terms

Main question: What really held the caste system in place?

  • Option 1: Religious beliefs about purity, duty, and rebirth.
  • Option 2: Economic interests, meaning control over work, land, and wealth.
  • Option 3: Caste was looser in real life than the religious law books claimed, and it became strict slowly.

Chapter 5 · 600 B.C.E.–600 C.E.

5.2 · State, Landlords, and Peasants in Han China

Who held the upper hand in Han China's social order?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

Who held the upper hand in Han China's social order?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 5.2.1 The imperial state: the Han state acted as a counterweight to great landowning families through land limits, state monopolies, restrictions on merchants, and reforms such as Wang Mang's, protecting the small farmer as the base of the empire.
  2. 5.2.2 The scholar-gentry: officials and great landlords were largely the same families, so state and elite formed a single ruling class that extracted wealth from peasants.
  3. 5.2.3 Pressure from below: peasant flight, debt, banditry, and rebellions such as the Red Eyebrows and Yellow Turbans set the real limits on both state and landlords and repeatedly reshaped Chinese politics. Contested
Keep in focus: Stay within 206 B.C.E.-220 C.E. Put one social group at the center of your argument and show how the others related to it.
In Plain Terms

Main question: In Han China, who was really in control of society?

  • Option 1: The government, which tried to protect small farmers from rich landowners.
  • Option 2: Rich educated landowners, who also ran the government.
  • Option 3: Peasants, whose rebellions forced the powerful to respond.

Chapter 5 · 600 B.C.E.–600 C.E.

5.3 · Understanding Greco-Roman Slavery

What was most fundamental about slavery in the Greek and Roman world?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

What was most fundamental about slavery in the Greek and Roman world?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 5.3.1 Its economic role: classical Athens and Roman Italy were true "slave societies" in which enslaved labor produced the wealth of the elite, especially after Rome's conquests flooded Italy with captives.
  2. 5.3.2 Its violence: slavery was fundamentally a relationship of domination and "social death," maintained by brutality and the constant fear of resistance and revolt.
  3. 5.3.3 Its openness: frequent manumission and a large, influential class of freed people made ancient slavery a porous institution very different from later racial slavery, though historians debate whose interests that openness served.
Keep in focus: Stay within about 500 B.C.E.-400 C.E. Be explicit whenever you compare ancient slavery with later Atlantic slavery.
In Plain Terms

Main question: What mattered most about slavery in ancient Greece and Rome?

  • Option 1: The economy depended on enslaved workers.
  • Option 2: Slavery was built on violence and fear.
  • Option 3: Many enslaved people were freed, so ancient slavery was less permanent than later slavery.

Every source in the four readers that bears on this prompt, with how it relates to each position: supports, qualifies, challenges, or counters (a Counter-Argument to answer). Reader pages are the printed page numbers in the reader. Select an identifier to open the source in The Garza Archive.

5

Chapter 5 · 600 B.C.E.–600 C.E.

5.4 · Comparing Patriarchies

How similar were the patriarchies of the second-wave world, and how much room did women have to act within them?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

How similar were the patriarchies of the second-wave world, and how much room did women have to act within them?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations, comparing at least two civilizations (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 5.4.1 A shared and durable structure: across Greece, Rome, India, and China, patriarchy took strikingly similar forms and generally became more restrictive as these civilizations matured. Contested
  2. 5.4.2 Meaningful variation: differences between Athens and Sparta, Rome and Han China, and elite and ordinary women were large enough to change what women's lives were actually like.
  3. 5.4.3 Agency within limits: through religious vocations, property, family influence, writing, and even public protest, women shaped their own lives and sometimes the societies around them.
Keep in focus: Stay within 600 B.C.E.-600 C.E. and compare at least two civilizations. Distinguish texts written by men about women from women's own words.
In Plain Terms

Main question: Was patriarchy the same everywhere, and could women do anything about it?

  • Option 1: Patriarchy was basically the same in every civilization and got stricter over time.
  • Option 2: Patriarchy looked very different from place to place, and those differences mattered.
  • Option 3: Women found ways to make choices and have influence even inside patriarchal societies.

Source Map

Every source in the four readers that bears on this prompt, with how it relates to each position: supports, qualifies, challenges, or counters (a Counter-Argument to answer). Reader pages are the printed page numbers in the reader. Select an identifier to open the source in The Garza Archive.

Prompt 15 of 22
5

Chapter 5 · 600 B.C.E.–600 C.E.

5.5 · Why Did Inequality Endure?

What made the deep inequalities of second-wave societies so durable?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

What made the deep inequalities of second-wave societies so durable?

Choose and defend one of the following explanations, comparing at least two societies (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 5.5.1 Ideas that justified it: religious and philosophical teachings such as dharma, Confucian hierarchy, and Aristotle's "natural slavery" made inequality seem natural, sacred, or right, even to many who suffered from it.
  2. 5.5.2 Force that enforced it: violence and the threat of violence, from slave punishments to the crushing of rebellions, kept the lower orders in place.
  3. 5.5.3 Economic structures that required it: agrarian economies in which landlords and states extracted surplus from peasants and slaves made inequality structural rather than a matter of ideas or force alone.
Keep in focus: Stay within 600 B.C.E.-600 C.E. and compare at least two of China, India, and the Greco-Roman world. Explain both why inequality persisted and how people challenged it.
In Plain Terms

Main question: Why did huge gaps between rich and poor, free and enslaved last so long?

  • Option 1: Religious and philosophical ideas convinced people inequality was right or natural.
  • Option 2: The powerful used violence to keep everyone else down.
  • Option 3: The economy was built so that a few people lived off the work of many.

Source Map

Every source in the four readers that bears on this prompt, with how it relates to each position: supports, qualifies, challenges, or counters (a Counter-Argument to answer). Reader pages are the printed page numbers in the reader. Select an identifier to open the source in The Garza Archive.

Prompt 16 of 22
6

Chapter 6 · 600 B.C.E.–1200 C.E.

6.1 · Isolated or Connected?

To what extent did the societies of Africa, the Americas, and Pacific Oceania develop in isolation?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

To what extent did the societies of Africa, the Americas, and Pacific Oceania develop in isolation?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 6.1.1 Largely independent invention: agriculture, cities, and states in these regions arose from local roots; even Africa's Niger Valley urbanism was an indigenous development rather than an import. Contested
  2. 6.1.2 Shaped by long-distance connection: where contact existed, as in Meroe and Axum with the Nile, Red Sea, Mediterranean, and Indian Ocean worlds, it decisively shaped states, religion, and trade.
  3. 6.1.3 Regional networks mattered most: neither isolation nor intercontinental contact but dense regional exchange (the Hopewell Interaction Sphere, Teotihuacan-Maya ties, Andean religious horizons, Pacific voyaging) drove change. Contested
Keep in focus: Stay within about 600 B.C.E.-1200 C.E. Define "isolation" at a specific scale (intercontinental, regional, or local) before you answer.
In Plain Terms

Main question: Did these societies grow up cut off from the rest of the world?

  • Option 1: Yes, mostly; they invented farming, cities, and governments on their own.
  • Option 2: No; contact with Eurasia shaped places like Meroe and Axum in major ways.
  • Option 3: What mattered most was trade and contact with neighbors in their own region.

Source Map

Every source in the four readers that bears on this prompt, with how it relates to each position: supports, qualifies, challenges, or counters (a Counter-Argument to answer). Reader pages are the printed page numbers in the reader. Select an identifier to open the source in The Garza Archive.

Chapter 6 · 600 B.C.E.–1200 C.E.

6.2 · Is "Civilization" the Right Yardstick?

How should historians understand societies that did not build states and cities on the Eurasian model, such as Bantu Africa, Pacific Oceania, the Niger Valley, and much of North America?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

How should historians understand societies that did not build states and cities on the Eurasian model, such as Bantu Africa, Pacific Oceania, the Niger Valley, and much of North America?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 6.2.1 Constraints explain the difference: population size, environment, available crops and animals, and distance limited the growth of states and cities in these regions.
  2. 6.2.2 Alternative forms of complexity: societies such as Jenne-jeno's "cities without citadels" and Pacific chiefdoms organized large populations through shared or overlapping authority, showing that complexity does not require a centralized state.
  3. 6.2.3 Deliberate choice: many peoples knew about or experimented with centralized power and chose to avoid, limit, or dismantle it. Contested
Keep in focus: Stay within about 500 B.C.E.-1200 C.E. Question the word "lack" in the chapter's own framing, and say whether you accept it.
In Plain Terms

Main question: Should we judge societies without kings and big cities as less developed?

  • Option 1: Geography, population, crops, and animals held them back from building states.
  • Option 2: They were complex in a different way, with shared power instead of a single ruler.
  • Option 3: Many of them chose not to have powerful rulers on purpose.

6

Chapter 6 · 600 B.C.E.–1200 C.E.

6.3 · The Classic Maya Collapse

What best explains the collapse of Classic Maya kingdoms in the southern lowlands between about 750 and 950 C.E.?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

What best explains the collapse of Classic Maya kingdoms in the southern lowlands between about 750 and 950 C.E.?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 6.3.1 Environmental crisis: prolonged drought, intensified by deforestation and pressure on farmland, undermined the agricultural base of dense city-states.
  2. 6.3.2 Political failure: endemic warfare among rival kings, the costs of elite display, and the loss of faith in divine kingship broke the political system.
  3. 6.3.3 Transformation, not collapse: royal courts fell, but Maya people, culture, and trade persisted and shifted north to centers such as Chichen Itza; "collapse" misdescribes the change.
Keep in focus: Stay within about 750-1000 C.E. Distinguish the collapse of royal courts from the fate of the Maya people.
In Plain Terms

Main question: Why did the great Maya cities of the south fall apart?

  • Option 1: Long droughts and damage to the environment made farming fail.
  • Option 2: Constant wars between kings and loss of trust in rulers destroyed the political system.
  • Option 3: The Maya did not disappear; power and people moved north and life went on in new forms.

Source Map

Every source in the four readers that bears on this prompt, with how it relates to each position: supports, qualifies, challenges, or counters (a Counter-Argument to answer). Reader pages are the printed page numbers in the reader. Select an identifier to open the source in The Garza Archive.

Prompt 19 of 22

Chapter 6 · 600 B.C.E.–1200 C.E.

6.4 · Power at Teotihuacan

Who ruled Teotihuacan, and how? Choose and defend one of the following interpretations of the largest city in the Americas before 600 C.E.

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

Who ruled Teotihuacan, and how? Choose and defend one of the following interpretations of the largest city in the Americas before 600 C.E.

Choose and defend one of the following positions (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 6.4.1 Powerful rulers and a state religion: monumental building, planned avenues, and mass sacrificial burials show strong, centralized, religiously sanctioned authority.
  2. 6.4.2 Collective governance: the absence of ruler portraits and royal tombs, the prominence of apartment compounds, and the city's corporate art point to rule by councils or cooperating groups. Contested
  3. 6.4.3 Change over time: early autocratic rulers gave way, after about 350 C.E., to a more collective political order.
Keep in focus: Stay within about 100 B.C.E.-650 C.E. The absence of certain evidence (no known ruler images or inscriptions) is itself part of the debate; explain how you use it.
In Plain Terms

Main question: Did kings run Teotihuacan, or was power shared?

  • Option 1: Strong rulers backed by religion ran the city.
  • Option 2: Groups or councils shared power, which is why no ruler pictures survive.
  • Option 3: The city started with strong rulers and later switched to shared power.

Source Map

Every source in the four readers that bears on this prompt, with how it relates to each position: supports, qualifies, challenges, or counters (a Counter-Argument to answer). Reader pages are the printed page numbers in the reader. Select an identifier to open the source in The Garza Archive.

How did Bantu-speaking peoples come to occupy much of Africa south of the equator, and what happened to the peoples already living there?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 6.5.1 Migration and displacement: farming, ironworking, and faster population growth allowed Bantu-speaking communities to move into new regions and largely replace or push aside forager peoples.
  2. 6.5.2 Gradual diffusion and exchange: languages, crops, and techniques spread through centuries of contact, intermarriage, and absorption, with many newcomers and older residents merging into new communities.
  3. 6.5.3 The agency of first-comers: Batwa and other forest peoples shaped the newcomers' knowledge of the land, religious ideas, and political legitimacy, so the expansion was a two-way process.
Keep in focus: Stay within about 1000 B.C.E.-1000 C.E. Historical linguistics and archaeology are the main evidence here; explain how you read them.
In Plain Terms

Main question: How did Bantu speakers spread across so much of Africa, and what happened to the people who were already there?

  • Option 1: Bantu farmers moved in and replaced the hunter-gatherers.
  • Option 2: Bantu ideas, crops, and language spread slowly as groups mixed together.
  • Option 3: The forest peoples already there taught and shaped the newcomers.

Chapter 6 · 600 B.C.E.–1200 C.E.

6.6 · What Held North America's Great Centers Together?

What held North America's largest early communities (Hopewell, Chaco Canyon, and Cahokia) together?

Ways of the World · Unit 2 · Second Wave Civilizations

What held North America's largest early communities (Hopewell, Chaco Canyon, and Cahokia) together?

Choose and defend one of the following interpretations, comparing at least two (or stake out a qualified position between two):

  1. 6.6.1 Political hierarchy: powerful leaders or chiefly elites mobilized labor, controlled resources, and, at Cahokia, staged sacrifices that display coercive power.
  2. 6.6.2 Religious ceremony and pilgrimage: great houses, earthworks, and mounds were ritual centers aligned with the sky and landscape that drew people together periodically for shared ceremony.
  3. 6.6.3 Exchange and cooperation without rulers: wide networks of trade and shared ritual linked communities that remained largely self-governing, without permanent coercive rulers. Contested
Keep in focus: Stay within about 200 B.C.E.-1250 C.E. and compare at least two of Hopewell, Chaco, and Cahokia. Treat Native oral traditions as evidence with their own rules of interpretation.
In Plain Terms

Main question: What brought thousands of people together at places like Chaco and Cahokia?

  • Option 1: Powerful leaders who could make people work and obey.
  • Option 2: Religion and ceremonies that drew people to sacred centers.
  • Option 3: Trade and cooperation between communities that governed themselves.

Every source in the four readers that bears on this prompt, with how it relates to each position: supports, qualifies, challenges, or counters (a Counter-Argument to answer). Reader pages are the printed page numbers in the reader. Select an identifier to open the source in The Garza Archive.
